Beam-and-block floors (report #134, LVK/Ludek): the Wienerberger POT joists with MIAKO ceramic blocks and a cast topping, made a floor type kind (decision 556, taken for the user) rather than an assembly of separate elements, so it is drawn with the Floor tool, typed, scheduled and edited like any floor. `BeamBlockSpec` (the type's `beamBlock`: the joist as a parametric inverted T — foot width and height, web width, height — its spacing and material; the block's height, length and material; the topping's thickness and material; the span the short way, along x or y or at an angle; the bearing into the supports; the mesh by name) with four indicative presets (POT + MIAKO 19/62.5, 19/50, 23/62.5, 25/50); the depth, block and topping, is derived and made the type's core when it is set (`architecture.setBeamBlock`, or `beamBlock` on `createSlabType`/`updateSlabType`, one step), the layers over it kept; setting it takes a timber construction off the type and the wood module's commands take it off in turn. `BeamBlock.Layout` lays the joists at the spacing across the floor, centred, each from edge to edge of the outline and on by the bearing, stopped short of an opening by a cast header a joist's foot wide; between them rows of whole blocks from the row's start and one cut at its end where both joists carry it. `Slabs.Geometry` then replaces the core with a part per joist (`bb-joist:n`, its T swept along it), the whole blocks as one part of copies of one block (`Unit`/`Instances`, IFC mapped items), the cut blocks, and the cast concrete as the core less the joists and the rows of blocks (topping, ribs, side strips and the frame round an opening in one solid; without manifold the topping alone). Nothing is stored: the layout is derived on every ask, so the parts' keys and GlobalIds stay when the floor is rebuilt. A floor's own `spanAngle` (`architecture.setFloorSpan`: an angle, or across an edge the joists bear on) overrides the type's; the plan draws the one-way span arrow and, at fine detail, the joists' axes; sections cut the parts by their materials. Quantities (`Lapin_BeamBlockQuantities`: joists and their length, whole and cut blocks, cast concrete, area, mesh area, and a set per material), the **Beam and block floors** schedule (joists by ordered length in 250 mm steps), IFC (the slab's parts: IfcBeam JOIST, IfcBuildingElementPart "Infill block" and "Cast concrete topping"). The user's catalogue comes from a CSV (`architecture.importBeamBlockTypes`, a type per row; `beamBlockPresets` serves the template). Floor types gain **Kind: Layered | Beam and block** with the fields and the engine's preview (`beamBlockPreview`: a sample floor with an opening in plan, two bays in section, the counts); a floor's properties show its span direction. Not done: doubled joists beside an opening, the rebar module's mesh as bars (the mesh is a name and an area), joists' camber and the makers' load tables. Tests: `BeamBlockTests`, `beamBlock.test.ts`; scene `beam-block-type`.
Beam types imported from a table, and beam types as lintels (report #135, LVK/Ludek): the tester asked to bring structure types (beams) in from a producer's CSV and to use them as the lintels of doors and windows, so the bearing is not typed on every rule and the lintels and the beam types are one list. Structure types has **Import…** and **Export CSV** under its list (`Lapin.Structure/TypeTables`, `structure.previewTypes`, `structure.importTypes`, `structure.typesCsv`): one row a type (name, kind beam, column or lintel, profile or h and b, material, mark, bearing, manufacturer, code, lengths, codes), the columns guessed from their headings in English, Romanian, German, French and Czech (the diacritics folded by a table of its own, as the browser engine runs without culture data) and a producer's export read by its `x.value` and `x.unit` columns (the unit of each row); the profile found in the project's copies, in the profile libraries the interface sends (this computer's and the shared folders') and in Lapin's catalogue, else a rectangle of h × b, a row with an unknown profile shown as not read. Rows of one section, material and maker that differ by their length can be one type (the name cut to what the names share up to a word's end: „KP 7 - 100”, „KP 7 - 125” → „KP 7”) with its stock lengths and the code of each. The preview lists each type as new, the same as the project's or differing (what differs), with the type choices of decision 506 (update, keep both, skip); one step to undo. A beam type keeps its lintel facts in its attributes (`Lapin.Platform/LintelTypes`: lengths, codes, code, manufacturer, bearing, mark; PredefinedType LINTEL for a lintel), shown and changed in Structure types. A lintel rule takes `beamType` (and `ownBearing`): `LintelRule.Resolve` reads the type every time the wall is built (so the walls follow the type) for the section, the lengths (none: cut to length), the material unless the rule names one, and the bearing unless the rule has its own; a type gone leaves the rule's stored product copy. The lintels stay derived pieces of their wall (decision 34): lighter, one rule edits them all, their GlobalIds stay the wall's, and IFC already writes each as an IfcBeam LINTEL part of the wall; the part now carries its type (`GeometryPart.TypeId`) and the export relates it to its IfcBeamType (LINTEL) by IfcRelDefinesByType. The lintel schedule lists them by the type's name and the code of the length taken, marked by the type's mark (P7, or P7-1, P7-2… when the type comes in several lengths). The lintel catalogue's import (`architecture.importLintelProducts`) also makes each product a beam type (the type of its code changed in place on a new import) when the product has the structure module; the rule editor lists the beam types first and the older catalogue's products that no type has. Old projects are unchanged: a rule without `beamType` is read as before, so no upgrade. Tests: `BeamTypeLintelTests`; vitest `lintels.test.ts`, `typeTables.test.ts`, `lintelFields.test.ts`; scene `beam-type-lintels`.

Circular vacuum lifts (tester's report #149, Rachel; decision 558): a pneumatic vacuum lift for houses is the drive `vacuum` of the transport module's elevator (`VacuumLift.cs`, docs/plan-elevators.md), placed by the Elevator tool in one of three indicative styles (one person Ø 750, two persons Ø 933, a wheelchair Ø 1300 with a swing door) and configured in the elevator configurator, whose plan and section come from the code that builds it. No shaft and no pit: a polycarbonate or glass tube on a base ring, a ring beam in each floor it passes (the floors open in circles by `Chords`, up to the head unit's top), a head unit on top, frame columns (two beside the lowest door, one in another landing's door left out and said), and at each landing a curved sliding, swing or no door with its own frame, facing the lift's door angle or its own (`doorAngles`, by storey); the cabin follows the tube's diameter until typed, with a curved sliding door or none; the frame, tube and cabin take colours of their own. Every piece is a closed prism. Plans draw the cut tube and columns, the cabin's circle with its cross, the doors' panels and slides or leaves and swings; the schedule lists it with its tube; IFC writes it as IfcTransportElement ELEVATOR with Pset_TransportElementCommon and reads it back native. Tests: `VacuumLiftTests`; the guide's Elevators page has its section and the scene `vacuum-lift`.


Trailing zeros in dimensions (forum topic 81, the tester Rychlas): Drafting standards → Project has "Suppress trailing zeros" (`StyleBook.SuppressZeros`, `standards.options {suppressZeros}`, carried by a standards file's options), and each dimension style a "Trailing zeros" column next to Decimals (`DimStyle.Zeros`: as the project, the default; keep; suppress; overridable per dimension as `o.zeros`). `Standards.FormatDimension` takes the resolved choice, so linear, aligned, radius, diameter, arc-length and ordinate values and the opening heights under a segment all follow, on screen, sheets, PDF and DXF (one formatter, the engine's drawings); feet and inches are fractions and keep theirs. Off by default, so drawings read as before. Leading zeros (DIMZIN's 0.5 → .5) were not added: the standards model no such CAD setting. Levels (`FormatLevel`, always two decimals as Romanian drawings write them) and areas (`AreaRounding`, its own step) have their own formatters and are unchanged; the interface's lengths already drop trailing zeros. Tests: `AnnotationTests.Trailing_zeros_are_written_unless_the_project_or_the_style_suppresses_them`.

Room colours with an opacity (forum topic 79, the tester Rychlas, for plans on the dark theme): a colour scheme's entry has an `opacity` (0–100 %, null or 100 opaque; `SchemeEntry.Opacity`, `setColorSchemeEntry {opacity}`), a room its own `fillOpacity` over its entry's. Drawings carry the fill as CSS writes it, `#rrggbbaa`: `HexColor` reads the eight digits (its components as the colour's, `Alpha`, `Opaque`, `WithAlpha`), so every reader of a colour keeps working; the plan's room fill and the colour legend's swatches carry it, the screen (`DrawingLayer`, `splitAlpha`) draws the colour at that `fill-opacity`, the SVG/HTML export writes `fill-opacity`, and the PDF fills through an ExtGState of that alpha (`/ca`, the same states the pictures use), so no "print opaque" choice was needed. The DXF (R2000) has no transparency and draws them opaque. UI: an Opacity column in Room colours, Own opacity in a room's properties. Tests: `RoomTests.A_scheme_entry_s_opacity_is_carried_by_the_plan_s_fill_the_legend_svg_and_pdf`, `screenInk.test.ts`.

Renumber axes (forum topic 75, the tester Rychlas): `drafting.renumberAxes` labels a grid's axes again in order across their family (`Grids.Across`: towards +x, or +y for axes along x), the ones named (`tags`, one direction) or all of a direction (`direction` x: the axes running up the plan, left to right; y: across it, bottom to top), in `numbers` (leading zeros kept: 01, 02) or `letters` (I and O left out unless `skipIO` is false: H → J, Z → AA) from `start`, after a `prefix`; a label another axis keeps is refused; a driven grid (a hall's) is refused. Axes are still keyed by their tag (dimension ends hold `{kind: grid, grid, tag}`), so the command and `updateAxis`'s `newTag`, which until now orphaned the dimensions on a renamed axis, both call `Grids.Retag`: one pass over the model that moves every grid end and every `grid#tag` string of that grid to the new tags, in the same undo step. Columns hold no reference to axes, so nothing else had to follow. UI: Annotate → Datum → Renumber axes… (also in an axis's and the grid's properties and the axis tool's bar), a dialog with the axes (the selected ones, or a direction), labels, start, prefix and "leave out I and O"; the selection follows the new tags. Tests: `GridRenumberTests`, `gridSpec.test.ts`.

Any type duplicated, and types in an order of the user's (forum topic 71, the tester Rychlas): core's `TypeOrder` adds `types.duplicate` (any type: its attributes copied with a new id, so a new GlobalId, named „Name (2)”, then the next free number; its own material set, a layer, profile or constituent set, copied with it so the copy is edited apart; materials, classifications and the rest shared; listed just after the original), `types.move` (up or down, `by`), `types.order` (a class's whole order) and the query `types.ordered`. The order is the project's `typeOrder` (`{class: [ids]}`), not an attribute of each type, so moving a type changes no type and rebuilds none of its elements; a type it does not name comes after those it names, in creation order, so projects that never ordered their types list them as before. The interface reads it in `ofClass` for every class of types, so every type list and picker (the dialogs, the properties panel's type, the tools' New element) follows. Duplicate / ↑ / ↓ (`TypeListActions`) are in Wall types, Floor types, Door and window types, Stair types and Structure types (columns, beams, footings); the other type dialogs read the order but keep their own Duplicate (sunshades, gates, furniture). Roofs have no types. Tests: `TypeOrderTests`, `typeOrder.test.ts`.

Right-click menu and dimension grips (forum topics 73, 74, Rychlas): the selection's right-click menu (`shell/selectionMenu.ts`) gains **Select similar in this view / in the whole project** (same class and type, the class alone for an untyped element; in a plan only the level shown), a submenu **Select all with the same…** (type, width, core material, material, storey; the pure matching in `shell/similar.ts`), **Edit type…** (the types dialog of the tool that puts that type down) and **Previous selections** (the last ten, `shell/selectionHistory.ts`, a selection kept when it is cleared or replaced; command `app.previousSelection`, Ctrl+Alt+Z). `MenuItem` got `submenu`. Not done: Quick select with filters (no selection-filter dialog exists), and Hide / Isolate in plans and sections (the temporary hide is the 3D's looks; a view's `hiddenElements` is read by the engine but no command writes it, and an implicit plan has no view entity). Dimensions: Ctrl/⌘-click on a dimension line's end grip continues the chain (`Grip.extend`: DIMADD on the selected string), Alt-click on a measured point's grip takes it out (`Grip.altRemove`, only for a grip alone in its group); the right-click menu of dimensions offers **Split into single dimensions**, **Join selected dimensions** (`joinDimensions`) and **Align selected dimensions** (`modules/drafting/dimensionMenu.ts`: `moveDimension` of each parallel string onto the first one's line, one undo step, no engine change).
Cable trays and trunking (report #147, a tester on the web app asking how to insert trunking or a tray; decision 563): the open `mep` module draws cable carriers. The **Cable tray** tool (<kbd>CT</kbd>, MEP → Cable carriers) takes a polyline at the bar's elevation and the type chosen there (a project type, or one of thirteen built-in ones, indicative: perforated and solid trays, ladders, trunking, conduit), its legs laid at 45° steps; an end on another carrier joins it (`IfcRelConnectsPorts`), a tee inside its length, a cross where two meet across from each other, a reducer end to end at another size. `CableCarriers` holds the element (`IfcCableCarrierSegment` of an `IfcCableCarrierSegmentType`, its kind, size, thickness, inside bend radius and fill limit), its commands (`mep.drawCarrier`, `setCarrierPoints`, `setCarrierLevels`, `connectCarrier`, `disconnectCarrier`, `createCarrierType`, `updateCarrierType`, `setCarrierType`, `setCableCarrier`; the query `mep.carrierFill`), the schedule `mep-carriers` and its ports for IFC (`CarrierNetwork`, CABLECARRIER); `CarrierBodies` sweeps the profile along the centreline, each piece mitred onto the next (a tray's open U, a ladder's rails and rungs, trunking's box and lid, conduit's tube by `Chords`), each corner a bend round the inside radius, the fittings parts keyed `fitting:…`, worked out on every ask. The IFC export writes those parts as `IfcCableCarrierFitting`s (BEND, TEE, CROSS, REDUCER) aggregated in the carrier, whose own body is its legs, and knows both classes' predefined types. A cable run's `carrier` says which carrier it lies in (Properties → Carrier, or Lay in carrier); the carrier's section in Properties shows its type, size and fill against its limit. The guide's MEP page has a "Cable trays and trunking" section and the scene `mep-carriers`. Tests: `MepCarrierTests`, `carriers.test.ts`.

Materials for rendering, the rest of report #76 (Timo, an ArchiCAD user rendering in Twinmotion; decision 564, after 288). Relief maps: a material names a normal map (`normalImage`) and a bump map (`bumpImage`), images of the project like its own texture (`Images.MaterialKeys`; one taken off goes when nothing uses it), laid on at its `textureSize` and turned by its `textureRotation`, with `normalStrength` and `bumpStrength` (0–10, 1 by default) in `MaterialLook`. Manage → Materials shows them under Own image (Normal map, Bump map, each with Import image…, Strength and the bin; a first map sets the size from the picture's proportion when the material has none), the preview sphere and the 3D view draw them (three's normalMap and bumpMap, loaded as data; a part with a relief map and no texture gets its box coordinates), and the furniture library carries them with its materials. The exports: glTF/GLB `normalTexture` with its scale; USD a raw UsdUVTexture into UsdPreviewSurface's `normal` (scale and bias by the strength), packed in the USDZ; OBJ/MTL `norm` for the normal map and `map_Bump -bm` for the bump map (the normal map as `map_Bump` too when there is no bump map, where Blender reads it); glTF has no bump map and COLLADA writes no images, so they leave them out. IFC import: a surface style's transparency (IfcSurfaceStyleShading's in IFC4, IfcSurfaceStyleRendering's in both schemas) comes in: on a material's representation it becomes the material's transparency (one the import makes, or one without its own), and on a body's styled item (`IfcModel.Colour` gives such a colour as #rrggbbaa) the reference's part keeps its colour and takes a material named after the style with that transparency (`IfcImport.Stored`, as the Fragments import does, decision 323); the file itself is never written. IFC export: a door or window of several materials gets an `IfcMaterialConstituentSet` (Door, Window) with a constituent per part of its joinery (`JoineryConstituents`: Frame, Sash, Glazing, Panel, Board, Hardware), shared by the elements with the same parts and materials and associated to the element and its type; the import reads a filler's (or its type's) constituent set back into its recognised type's `JoineryMaterials` (IFC4's Lining and Framing too), the parts the file does not name taking Lapin's shared materials. The guide: Materials (Normal and bump maps; the IFC notes) and Import/export in English and Romanian, and a new page False ceilings (`ceilings.md`, both languages, scene `ceilings-guide`). Tests: `MaterialImageTests` (relief maps saved and opened, in GLB, OBJ, USD/USDZ; a normal map alone), `MaterialAppearanceTests` (the constituent set and its reimport), `IfcRecognitionTests.Materials` (a see-through material and body, a window's constituents), `look.test.ts`.



**View crop and annotation crop; Hide in view, temporary hide/isolate and Reveal hidden in 2D views; Quick select (14.11.2026, forum topics 68 and 73, testers Victor1 and Rychlas).** #68: a plan, section, elevation or reference plane keeps `crop: {on, visible, min, max, annotation, offset}` (`ViewCrops`), its corners in the view's turned frame (a turned plan's region square to the screen). `view.setCrop` (one step; turned on with no region, the box round what the view draws plus 0.5 m; a level's default plan saved as its plan view through `Views.OwnView`, which also serves hiding). `Views.Render` cuts the model's pens to the region (`Active.Clip`, Sheets' clip routines made internal), the model's marks (pick lines, rooms' fills under the model, pictures) too, and the annotation marks to the annotation crop when it is on (offsets in paper mm): a mark wholly outside goes, one across it is cut, a text kept by its point; the frame (layer `CROP`, a mark owned by the view, so a click selects it and `cropGrips` drag it) only when visible, printed then; the bounds are the region. A sheet's free viewport takes the region (or the annotation crop) with no margin (`Sheets.Place`). View settings: Crop view, Crop region visible, the corners, Annotation crop and its offsets (`ViewCrop.tsx`). #73: `view.hideElements {viewId, ids}` and `view.unhideElements {viewId, ids | all}` (`ViewHiding`, one step each, the `hiddenElements` every drawing and module already read); the temporary hide and isolate of a 2D view and Reveal hidden are the interface's (`viewTemporary.ts`, per view, not saved), sent with `view.drawing` as `temp: {hide, isolate}` and `reveal` and applied on a copy of the view (`ViewHiding.Temporary`: isolate hides every element on a storey but those), so the one resolver draws them; revealed elements are drawn in `#d33fb0`. HH, HI, HR act on the 2D view in front, the 3D view's as before; the right-click menu of plans and drawings gains Hide in view, Hide for now, Isolate for now, Reset temporary hide/isolate, Reveal hidden, Unhide in view and Quick select… (`viewHideMenu.ts`, its own provider). Quick select: `select.query {rules, match, viewId?, temp?, reveal?}` → ids, derived on every ask, the rules a view filter's (`ViewFilters.Rules`, made public, with a new `between` operator, "min..max", either end open); in a view, what its drawing's marks name (crop, hidden and temporary state counted). Dialog `QuickSelectDialog` (View → Select → Quick select, QS, and the right-click menu). Tests: `ViewCropTests`, `ViewHidingTests`, `quickSelect.test.ts`, `viewTemporary.test.ts`; guide: views (Crop region), interface (Hide in a plan or a section, Quick select), scenes `views-crop`, `interface-quick-select`.

The Parameters inspector (forum topic 84, Rychlas): the fields and formulas could read a great deal (an element's own fields, its parameters, its quantities, and through its scopes its storey, project, type, host and rooms, decision 551) but nothing told a user the names. The query `fields.available {id}` (`FieldCatalog`, core) lists every field an element's tags, live texts and formulas can read, grouped: the fields every element has, its attributes (its class's fields by their labels, any other text, number or yes/no it keeps, not ids nor Lapin's bookkeeping), what its module works out, its quantities (formulas only), its parameters (a formula's worked out), the standard IFC properties of its class and those it came with, the project's parameters by their key, then each scope it has with that scope's name, mark, attributes, worked-out fields and parameters; each with the token to write, its value now (`TagText.Value`, so it is what a tag shows) and a few words on what it is. The interface's Parameters inspector (`shell/FieldsInspector.tsx`, model in `fieldsModel.ts`) shows it with a search: from Properties → Parameters → Parameters inspector (a dialog; a click copies `{token}`) and from a formula's **{ }** button (Insert field…, inline under the formula; a click inserts `{token}` in a text with fields, the bare name, its spaces taken out, in an expression, `{= Name}` for a quantity). Modules add fields worked out for a class (`DerivedFields`, read by `TagText.Value` after the element's own parameters and attributes, and by `ParamFormulas.Read`, never stored): a room's `finish.name`, `finish.floor` (its finish's top floor layer's material), `finish.walls` and `finish.ceiling` (the layer facing the room), and `finishFloor`, which a room's own floor finish overrides, so an empty Floor finish inherits its finish's floor (shown greyed in the room's properties; the Room tag dialog offers the four finish fields). A tag's plain field now also reads a quantity the caller has (`{NetArea}` in a template formula). Tests: `FieldCatalogTests`; UI `fieldsModel.test.ts`; guide: interface.md (The Parameters inspector), rooms.md (floor finish inherited, a room's fields from its finish), scene `fields-inspector`.

Room separation lines (forum topic 80, Rychlas): a line drawn in plan that bounds rooms as a wall would, with no body (an open-plan kitchen and living room, two ends of a hall). It is an `IfcVirtualElement`, PredefinedType BOUNDARY (`RoomDividers` in the platform, `RoomDividerElements` in architecture), its shape kept as drafting keeps one (line, polyline, circle, arc; a rectangle is a closed polyline, an arc is given by its start, its end and a point on it), contained in its storey. `RoomBounds` adds each divider of the storey to the free space as a strip 0.1 mm to either side of each straight piece (arcs and circles in chords, `Chords`), an open line's ends reaching 5 mm past themselves so one drawn to a wall's face closes on it; then `RoomDividers.Exact` puts the region's corners on a strip back on the divider's line (two pieces' meeting point at a corner or a chord, along the wall's face where the strip meets a wall, else across), takes out the out-and-back spike a line ending in the room leaves and drops an island with no area, so rooms meet exactly on the line and their areas, perimeters and volumes add up to the undivided room (a 6 × 4 m room split down the middle is two of exactly 12 m²; a line stopping in the room changes nothing). The rooms placed by a click find their outlines again on every build (the cache reads the class, so a divider drawn, moved or deleted re-bounds them; nothing is stored on the rooms). Commands `architecture.addRoomDivider {storeyId, kind, points | shape}` and `updateRoomDivider`, one undo step each; a linework converter `roomDivider` (Convert to… → Room separation lines). Drawn in its storey's plans as a thin dashed line (`Generator.SymbolicInPlan`), its length its quantity. IFC: an IfcVirtualElement (no PredefinedType in IFC4, BOUNDARY in IFC 4.3) with its line as the FootPrint Curve2D representation and its shape in Lapin_Intent, read back as a divider. UI: Architecture → Room → Room separation, a pulldown of line (RS, chained), polyline, rectangle, arc and circle; Create similar draws the same kind. Tests: `RoomDividerTests`; guide: rooms.md (Room separation lines), drafting.md, scene `room-dividers`.


Levels with their own decimals and the trailing-zeros option (decision 568, the user's follow-up to forum topic 81): Drafting standards → Project has "Level decimals" (0–4, 2 by default; `StyleBook.LevelDecimals`) and "Level trailing zeros" (as the project, the default, following "Suppress trailing zeros"; keep; suppress; `StyleBook.LevelZeros`), both `standards.options` arguments carried by a standards file's options. One formatter, `Standards.FormatLevel(z, decimals, suppress)` and `FormatLevel(z, book)`, now writes every level a drawing prints: the level marks across sections and elevations (Lapin's, still +0.00 at zero, and a `levelHead` symbol's `{level.elevation}`), spot levels (`{point.elevation}`), a plan's `{level.elevation}`, level dimensions (which may carry their own `levelDecimals`/`levelZeros`, given to `drafting.addDimension` as `decimals`/`zeros`; a dimension style's decimals and zeros do not apply to them), the base and survey points, terrain and pad levels, and a coordination void's bottom level. Signs as before (+3.00, −0.15, ±0.00); metres in a metric project whatever its unit, feet and inches in an imperial one. Defaults keep every drawing as it was. Tests: `AnnotationTests.Levels_take_the_project_s_decimals_and_trailing_zeros`; guide: Drafting → Dimensions, "Levels" (EN, RO).
A large SDNF file comes in quickly, with its progress and its protocol (report #148, Michael; decision 569): a large file left the tester on the desktop with the window slow to answer and nothing from the file, and no protocol. The import ran with no overlay while the engine worked (every click's call queued behind it) and took minutes, growing with the square of the frame: each member related to its storey and type one at a time (a relationship of thousands copied at each), each beam's span looking at every column and its cut reading every possible cutter, so 7,000 beams noted 52 million dependencies. Measured on a generated 11,096-member frame with 625 plates and 2,500 holes: 256 s before, 23 s after. The import now relates each storey's and type's elements in one go (`EditRelations.RelateAll`); past 500 columns or cutters of a class (`NearReads.WholeUpTo`; below it they are read whole, as before, so an undo still finds the earlier builds), a beam reads only the columns round its axis and an element only the cutters round its box (`NearReads` cells, the cutters of a revision indexed by cell once in `ModuleCatalog`). Its stages show in the busy overlay (reading, the members, the plates, the holes, the model), with Cancel (`cancel.long` now reaches any call through `Session.Cancellation`; the import stops and its edit is undone). A record that cannot be read is told with its line, packet and item (`SdnfFormatException`), and nothing comes in. At the end the SDNF import dialog shows the protocol: each packet's count (announced, read, imported), what was left out and changed grouped by reason with each item's number and line, the placeholder profiles, the notes and the time, with Copy and Save… as text. Tests: `SdnfLargeTests`, `sdnfReport.test.ts`; docs/sdnf.md, the user guide's SDNF section.

**Exterior dimensions and dimension rules (forum topic 78, decision 570).** A tester asked for an Exterior dimensions function beside Room dimensions, both with rules the user owns. `drafting.exteriorDimensions {viewId, wallIds?, settings?}` (Annotate → Exterior dimensions, ED, EXTDIM) finds the facades from the plan's cut (`ExteriorDimensions.Facades`: a wall face is outside when, a hair in front of it, a ray escapes the building, the openings' boxes closing the walls' gaps; outside faces in line with only openings' gaps between them are one facade) and lays parallel strings outside each, in the rules' order from the wall outward: details (corners, jambs), openings' centres, grid axes, rooms (the walls meeting the facade from inside, found on a line just inside its inner face), overall; measured to the walls' faces, their core (the core layer's cut) or their axes. Every point holds on what it measures: corners and partitions' faces as edge refs, axes as `axis`/`grid` refs, openings by a new ref kind `opening` (`Dimensions.HoldOnOpening`: its place from the opening's centre in the wall's frame, a jamb taken onto the wall's cut jamb face), which a dimension driven by its value also slides. Run again it replaces its own strings (`exteriorString`, `facadeWalls`) and keeps the user's. The rules (`DimensionRules`: strings and their order, offset and spacing in paper mm, dimension style, measure to, narrowest opening) live on the project (`dimensionRules`, `drafting.setDimensionRules`, `drafting.dimensionRules`), travel in `.lapinstandards` with its options, and are edited in Annotate → Dimension rules… (`DimensionRulesDialog`, two tabs). Room dimensions take the same rules where they apply: an optional overall string per side further in, their style, narrow openings left out; the defaults make what they made before. Tests: `ExteriorDimensionTests`; guide: drafting (Exterior dimensions and dimension rules), rooms.

The web build back under its budgets (decision 571, 14.11.2026). Release 0.1.1022's `pnpm build:web lapin` failed twice over: the first load was 16.0 MB (budget 15) and 18.0 with every lazy part (budget 17), and `check-web` saw `Lapin.Mesh`, a lazy part, fetched at every start. The fetch came from the furniture models of the user's own (decision 512, 4e2c42b3): `UserModels.Read` reads a GLB through Lapin.Mesh, and its lambdas over the mesh pieces shared the class's one closure class with the lambdas of the commands `UserModels.Register` adds at start; Mono's AOT sets up all that closure class's fields when the first lambda is cached, which loads Lapin.Mesh. The GLB reading is now a nested class of its own (`UserModels.Glb`). The size was the engine's own growth since 0.1.889 (`dotnet.native.wasm` 10.1 → 12.3 MB brotli), so `build-web.mjs` takes the lever weighed on 09.11.2026: 11 modules (planning, cost, clash, site works, rebar, wood, steel, MEP, civil, transport, graphs) left to the interpreter (`INTERPRETED`), still loaded and registered at start. The build measures 14.3 MB at the first load and 16.3 MB with everything; `check-web` passes with nothing lazy fetched to open a project or draw a wall. The budgets did not change (docs/plan-browser-engine.md, Lazy parts).

An opening cuts every wall it passes through (forum topic 64, decision 572): a door, window or bare opening also cuts each straight wall lying along its host — parallel, its body touching or overlapping the host's (stacked on it, under it, a lining against it, one in line) — where its box reaches into it, through that wall's thickness (`ThroughOpenings.Into`, found near the wall by `PlaceReads`: the store stamps cells of a 2 m grid by the walls' axes (`PlaceCells`), so an edit rebuilds only the walls round it and undo still takes back earlier builds; derived on every build, nothing stored). `Walls.Geometry` adds the boxes to its cutters, the plan gaps and the lintels (`LintelPieces`); `Openings.CheckFits` lets an opening run out of its host into the walls it cuts (`ThroughOpenings.Reach`; imports check within the host alone). The opening's `cutThrough` (default on, `architecture.setCutThrough`, a checkbox in the door/window and opening panels) turns it off. Walls recognised from a foreign IFC neither cut nor are cut; a Lapin file round-trips with the cut derived again. IFC export writes one more IfcOpeningElement + IfcRelVoidsElement per wall cut, GlobalIds derived from the opening and the wall (`IVoidShapes.Through`); `ArchitectureVoids` yields a void per wall too. Tests: `ThroughOpeningTests`; guide: doors-windows (Openings through several walls), EN and RO.

After a review of it: the store keeps each cell's elements (`ModelStore.InCell`), so `PlaceReads` gives a build the walls in its cells alone, not the whole class (a snapshot of 2000 walls 874 → 108 ms, 4000 walls 2557 → 270 ms; 91 and 184 ms before the feature); a cell's stamp is a sum over where its walls are (`PlaceCells.Hash`: id, axis, arc, base, height, top, thickness, imported), and an edit touches the cells only when a wall moves in them, so a renamed wall is the only one built again (it was every wall on the ten storeys over it); a wall on another storey or out of reach is not read at all, only looked at; axes written as integers are read (`Json.Number`); only walls are laid in cells, and an empty cell is dropped; `Walls.Geometry` works out the openings through it once for its cuts and its lintels; `CheckFits` looks only at walls parallel and near by their axes; another wall's axis is looked for up to the wall's half body and 1.5 m (walls up to 3 m thick). Tests added: kept builds through moves in and out, undo, redo, a delete and a batch; an integer move; a rename; a wall too long for cells; a snapshot of 2000 walls about four times that of 500.

The finish painter (decision 573, forum topic 83, docs/plan-finish-painter.md). A tester asked for a Finish Editor: beside the room finishes laid on whole rooms, faces divided into sections and painted by a brush, the building's outside above all. Stage 1: a finish zone (`LapinFinishZone`, exported as IfcCovering CLADDING, FLOORING or CEILING with `IfcRelCoversBldgElements`) belongs to a named face of a host (`IElementFaces`: a wall's two sides, a floor's top, soffit and edges, a column's or beam's sides), kept in the face's own frame so it follows the host, and goes with it when the host is deleted. Splits (`LapinFinishSplit`: lines, polylines, closed shapes, or a level line across an upright face) divide the face into regions by Studio's `SketchRegions`, moved into Lapin.Geometry for both; a zone with a seed paints the region holding it, one without the rest of the face, a bare one nothing. Its finish is a surface finish type (`LapinSurfaceFinishType`, a library of indicative build-ups: ETICS, lime plaster, tiles, stone, paint, gypsum board), a room finish's part (read on every ask), or layers of its own. Each owned region is built per layer from the face outward, the face's openings and the islands drawn in it cut out, mitred at 45° at a wall's outer corner; measured per layer (net, gross, volume), listed by the Surface finishes schedule, costed as finishes, cut and hatched in plans and sections by material. Commands `architecture.paintFinish` (whole, rest), `splitFinishFace`, `clearFinish`, the surface finish types; queries `finishFaceAt` and `finishPalette`. The interface: the painter's edit mode (Interior → Finish painter, FP; Room finishes → Finish painter…) with its palette, the brush (3D and plans, the region lit under the cursor), splits by rectangle and line drawn on the face in 3D (the direct sketch given an owner), the eyedropper, Clear and Whole face. Rooms keep measuring to their room finish. Tests: `FinishZoneTests`; guide: rooms (Finish painter), with its scene.

Guide run fixes (14.11.2026): the 3D view's floating command line let clicks through to the cards under it (empty history takes no room, only its own controls take the pointer, bottom cards sit above it); a walk's eye is no longer remembered as the 3D tab's camera (viewState3d), so the camera there was comes back; the codemirror and tiptap packages are pre-bundled by Vite (optimizeDeps.include), since met lazily they reloaded the page to the start screen when the Script panel first opened on a fresh cache.

**A 3D view on a sheet (forum topic 91, 14.11.2026, decision 574).** A saved 3D view placed on a sheet stayed empty and a double-click on it opened an empty drawing: the engine drew no 3D view (`Views.Spec` answered "A 3D view is not a drawing", so `Sheets.Place` got no pens), and the sheet opened every view that was not a plan or a schedule in the drawing pane. Now the engine draws it (`View3dDrawings`), with the same exact hidden-line removal as an elevation and through the one resolver (Visibility / Graphics, the view's hidden elements), plus the storeys it lists and its section box (parts across it cut by manifold): an orthographic camera is the whole model seen along its direction, an axonometric at the view's scale; a perspective maps the parts by the perspective (a projective map keeps faces planar and the order along each ray, so the orthographic hidden-line removal of the mapped parts is the perspective's), at true scale in the plane through the camera's target, cut at 0.1 m before the eye (parts across it cut by manifold, left out without it) and to the camera's field of view (`fov`, `aspect`, now kept by `view.setCamera` and sent by the browser's camera button; 45° and 16:9 otherwise). A view saved without a camera is seen from the south-west, from above, orthographic. Its drawing has lines only (no surface hatches, shadows or annotations, whose frame would not be the model's in perspective). The sheet, its PDF and DXF, and the drawing pane take it; a double-click or ✎ on it opens the 3D pane with the view and its camera (`viewport/sheetOpen.ts`). Tests: `Sheet3dViewTests` (7: orthographic, no camera, perspective foreshortening and field of view, inside the model cut at the eye, section box and hidden elements, saved and opened again, the PDF), `sheetOpen.test.ts`; guide: sheets (EN, RO).

Layer extents, stage 1 (forum topic 89, benlungu, 14.11.2026; docs/plan-layer-extents.md): a wall's or floor's layer goes on past the element's edge, or stops short of it, without splitting the element — the floor finish run 2 cm over the wall it was drawn to, a plaster down past the wall's base. Each extent is the element's own (`layerExtents`: layer, edge, offset, cuts; in Lapin_Intent), the type's layer set untouched: a straight wall's start, end, bottom and top, a floor's outline all round or one edge (followed when a corner is moved, added or taken away). What is short is taken off the layer where it is built; what is past is a piece of its own (`Walls.ExtentPieces`, `Slabs.ExtentPieces`), added after the cut priorities, cut by the openings and joined in one body with its layer, so plans (a wall's cut past its end, a floor's layer outlines), sections, 3D, quantities and IFC follow. **Cuts others** takes the piece out of the walls and floors it meets (`LayerExtents.CutBy`, read under a narrow key `extents:<class>` the session touches when extents change, stamped as the class for undo); off, the two overlap. Commands `architecture.setLayerExtent` and `clearLayerExtents`, one step each; the properties section **Layer extents**. Left: grips in sections and plans, curved walls, roofs, type defaults, cutting columns and beams. Tests: `LayerExtentTests` (10), `layerExtents.test.ts`; guide: walls (Layer extents), with its scene `layer-extents`.

Inset views anywhere (decision 576, 14.11.2026, the user's request: "in rebar we can load other views, editable, at their own scales, into a single viewport; I want it generally available, it is very useful for detailing"). Rebar's inset view (decision 211) was already a core annotation, `LapinInset` with `view.inset` / `view.updateInset` (Insets.cs): the source view rendered by the engine inside the host at its point, by a factor (by default the source's paper size), framed with its name and scale, depth 1, the source's own annotations coming through as marks of their owners with the mapping they came by (`via`); only its tool, its picker and its grips were rebar's, and only rebar's bars mapped their grips through it. Now: the tool is the core's (View → Inset view, IV; rebar's tab keeps it as `rebar.inset`, the same tool), in any plan, section, elevation or reference plane's view, offering every 2D view and each level's own plan (a 3D view refused as host or source); an inset keeps a scale of its own (`scale`, 1:N on paper, beside the kept `factor`), a crop of its own (`crop` {min, max} in the source's coordinates, or `true` round what it shows; its corners dragged in the view), and its properties section (view shown, scale, framed, crop). The source is rendered with the host's `print`, so a sheet, PDF or DXF of the host leaves out what the source shows on the screen only (its reference planes' names were printed before); the source's pick, edges and hit areas come through too. Editing through an inset is the platform's now (viewport/insetMap.ts): a grip any module makes for an element on the source view is shown where the inset shows it and its drag taken back (GripLayer; rebar's own mapped grips left as they are), a drafted shape dragged by its body through an inset moves on its source by the source's metres, CAD drafting (line, polyline, rectangle, circle, arc) over an inset is drawn on its source, and a plan's annotation tools (drafting, annotate, rebar) clicked over an inset draw on its source as a section's tools always did. Projects keep their insets unchanged (same class and attributes; no upgrade). Tests: `InsetViewTests` (scale, crop, live, the drafted line through it with its mapping, depth 1, undo, print and DXF), rebar's `AcrossSectionTests` kept; vitest `insetMap.test.ts`, `core/insets.test.ts`. Guide: views (Inset views), rebar pointing to it, scene `views-inset`.

Grid arrays and a clicked step (14.11.2026, forum topic 90, Petri; decision 577): Modify → Array gains the kind **In a grid**, `element.array {kind: "grid", count (the columns), step, rows, rowStep, levels, levelStep}`, kept as a `LapinArray` like the other kinds and edited from the Array section (columns, rows, levels and their steps). Each grid item keeps its cell (column, row, level) beside its ids, so more or fewer columns, rows or levels keep the ids of every cell still in the grid (a row turned into a grid keeps its copies as the first row); saved in the package, never written to IFC. A row's or a grid's step can be clicked in a plan (Step given by → Clicking two points in a plan, the command ARRAYSTEP): the first point, the second (a typed distance goes along the pointer's direction), and for a grid's rows a third, the selection's outline drawn live at every place. Studio's Array takes Rows too: a solid repeated in a grid (`array` step with `rows`, `rowStep`, `levels`, `levelStep`, neighbours face to face joined) and anything else as a grid array. The dialog's existing fields keep their places (the grid's come last). Tests: `ArrayGridTests`, `StudioEditorTests.A_solid_repeated_in_a_grid_of_columns_rows_and_levels`, `arrayGrid.test.ts`; guide: groups (Arrays) and studio, the scene `array-grid`.

Layer extents, stage 2 (14.11.2026, forum topic 89, benlungu; decision 578, docs/plan-layer-extents.md): **Edit layers** (Architecture → Edit layers, or the button under Properties → Layer extents), an edit scope where the selected wall's or floor's layers show their outlines and a grip on each edge, in plans and sections (where the cut crosses an edge, or runs through the layer for a wall's end), dragged with the usual snaps and a typed distance, one `setLayerExtent` per drop; the engine tells the edges as built (`architecture.layerEdges`), the views lend a section's frame to the grips (`GripView.frame`) and modules can refresh their grips (`bumpGrips`). Curved walls take extents at their start, end, bottom and top (along the arc short of an end, on the end's tangent past it); under a roof a layer going up to it follows the roof moved by its top extent (the gable included); wall and floor types hold default extents (the element's own win, an own 0 keeps the layer, `inherit` gives the place back), cutting holders include the elements of such types; the layers past their edges cut columns, beams and footings too (`ILayerExtentCuts`), and `cuts: "order"` cuts only what the element outranks by the cut order. Roofs have no layer set, so no extents (documented as a decision for the user). Nothing stored differently: no upgrade. Tests: `LayerExtentTests` (grips, curved walls, roofs, types and IFC, a column, the cut order), vitest `layerEdit.test.ts`; guide: walls and floors-roofs in all nine languages (the Layer extents section translated into es, de, fr, da, it, pl, pt), the scene `layer-extents` with `layer-edit-plan` and `layer-edit-section`.
Shop drawings, stage S0: element frames and element views (14.11.2026, forum topic 93, Victor1; decisions 579, 580; `docs/plan-shop-drawings.md`). Every element has its own frame, worked out on every ask and never kept (`ElementFrames`, `IElementFrames`): a module's axes first (steel members, beams and columns of a steel profile along their axis from where their holes are measured, the web up; a placed Studio part in its own axes), then the core's (a path element along its axis, a column or footing by its rotation), else one from the body (an axis within 1° of vertical snapped to it and the box of least area in plan, X along its long side; a tilted body by its principal axes; an `IfcPlate` with Z through its thickness). An element view is an ordinary view with `of: {element, role, station?, flip?, margin?, depth?}` (`ElementViews`): front, back, top, bottom, left and right are sections whose plane stands just outside the element's box, seeing through it; `section:x|y|z` cut it square to a frame axis at a station; `iso` is a 3D view from the frame's south-west, orthographic. Their drawing points are in the frame's axes about the box's middle, so a member turned 37° and tilted draws the same front as one along X, and a view follows its element when it moves. The view's **subject** (`subject: [ids]`, the element by default; `context: faded` greys the rest) is the first rule of `VisibilityGraphics`, so plans, sections, sheets, PDF, DXF, the 3D and the mesh exports all read it; a view's own drafting and annotations stay. `view.setSubject` keeps an isolate on any view (one step). The element-anchored views there were are roles (`IElementViewRoles`): the section across an element (`across`), the timber-frame or CLT wall's frame elevation (`wood.frame`), a steel hall's frame elevation and anchor plan (`steel.frame`, `steel.anchorPlan`), each made by its old command's code and keeping its keys, so saved projects open and draw unchanged (no upgrade needed). Commands and queries: `elementView.create`, `view.setSubject`, `elementView.roles`, `element.frame`. Found on the way: moving or copying a steel member in plan dropped its ends' heights and changed its roll (the platform's carry now keeps a point's height; steel's `MemberPlacement` keeps the roll). Tests: `ElementViewTests` (9).

Shop drawings, stage S1: a single part on a sheet (14.11.2026, forum topic 93, Victor1; decision 581, `docs/plan-shop-drawings.md`). **Make drawing…** (right-click an element, or Annotate → Shop drawing) opens a dialog with the part drawn by the engine in iso (`elementView.iso`) with its frame's axes and a check box on each of its six faces and Iso, sections square to its x, y or z added with a slider each (their station from the frame's origin), and on the right the rule, scale (auto or 1:N), paper (auto or A4–A0), projection (ISO E, the project's by default, `drafting.projection`; or third angle), dimensions (overall or none), what wins (paper or scale) and the live sheet the engine composes (`drawing.preview`, over an `OverlayModel`, nothing kept). **Create** (`drawing.create`, one step) makes a `LapinShopDrawing` (its element, rule, choices, sheet and views by role), its sheet in the Shop drawings discipline and its element views: the orthographic views round the front by the projection method, sections in a row under them, the iso at half the scale at the top right, the part's data (mark, kind, type, material, size, weight, quantity) in a table over the title block (an `ISheetContent` of the drawing itself); the first paper of the rule's list that holds them at the scale (auto: the shop series' first that draws the part about 110 mm long), else down the series; prefer scale: down the series on the paper asked first. An end view drawn under 20 mm and every section get a scale of their own. View titles are counted in the layout. Made again (`drawing.create` or `drawing.update`) it keeps the drawing's, sheet's, views' and viewports' ids and the user's own settings on the views (a name, hidden elements, graphics, drafting); views no longer asked for go with their drafting; `drawing.remove` takes the sheet and views. The views' overall dimensions are derived on every draw by Drafting's `PartDimensions` with the same code as a user's dimension (`Dimensions.Draw`, extracted), so they follow the part; the annotation category Dimensions hides them. Rules: the coded generic one (front, top, right, iso, overall dimensions), modules add theirs through `IDrawingRules`. Tests: `ShopDrawingTests` (8: first-angle places on A4, third angle, ids and edits kept, one undo, A3 or down the scales, dimensions following the part, the preview keeping nothing, a steel member's data), vitest `shopDrawing.test.ts`; guide: shop-drawings (EN, RO), the scene `shop-drawings`.

Shop drawings, stage S2: features, part dimensions, sections at features (14.11.2026, forum topic 93, Victor1; decision 582, `docs/plan-shop-drawings.md`). An element's features in its own frame (`ElementFeatures`, `IElementFeatures`): a module's (steel: a member's round holes from `MemberHoles`, the copes at its ends as notches, an end cut by a plane with its plan and elevation angles), else from its body (round holes as the inner loops of its faces on a circle, one per hole; what a plate from an SDNF or IFC file and a Studio part get). `element.features` lists them. Holes seen face on group in runs (one line across, one pitch, one diameter). Drafting's `PartDimensions` draws, with the dimensions mode **features** (`of.dims`, `dims: "features"` on `drawing.create`): the overall strings, a chain from end to end through each run's first and last hole with a run written "4×80 = 320", the ordinates of every hole from the part's start, the rows across on the left, each run's count and diameter ("5× Ø22"), a notch's length and depth, an end cut's angle in the view that sees it; all derived on every draw, so they follow the part; a string moved (`drawing.moveDimension {viewId, key, by}` or `reset`) keeps its move by its key on the view (`partDimMoves`). The drawing's option `atFeatures` adds a section through the middle of each notch (keyed `feature:<notch>`, so made again it keeps its id). Steel's rules: **Steel member** (front, top, iso, features, sections at notches) and **Steel plate** (face and edge view, features); an auto scale with features draws the part about 250 mm long; the views keep room over and beside them for the feature strings. `steel.addHoles` drills a run of round holes through a member's web or a flange (station, count, pitch, diameter, across), `steel.clearHoles` takes them away. The dialog offers Dimensions → Features (holes, notches) and Sections at the notches. Left for later (documented in the plan): details (insets) at features, section markers on the front view, wood and Studio features (their sketches), a plate's chamfers, mitred and fitted ends' angles, hole groups' gauge strings in flanges seen from above, dragging a derived string on screen (the command is there). Tests: `ShopFeatureTests` (5), vitest `shopDrawing.test.ts`.

Shop drawings, stage S3: marks of identical parts (14.11.2026, forum topic 93, Victor1; decision 583, `docs/plan-shop-drawings.md`). A part's **signature** (`Marks.Signature`): its class, materials and finish, and its body in its own (unturned) frame about the box's middle, every point rounded to 0.5 mm, the least of the four proper turns that keep its box, hashed: a copy turned or moved, or a member drawn from its other end, is the same part; a mirror image is not. Grouping is derived; the names are kept in the project's **register** (`marks` on the IfcProject: mark, prefix, number, signature, the parts that had it, the frame's turn), as GlobalIds are, nothing written into an element. `marks.number {ids?}` (none: every part a module gives a prefix, steel's, and every part with a drawing) keeps each signature's mark, gives a new one the next number of its prefix (the biggest parts first), keeps a mark whose parts all changed alike (the only part of it edited stays P12) and gives the edited parts of a mark that has others a new one (modified numbering); a mark whose drawing's sheet went out in a CDE issue is frozen (its parts edited take a new mark); a mark whose parts are gone stays free until `marks.renumber {prefix?}` (host only in a shared session: `EngineDispatcher.HostOnlyCommands`). `marks.setFrame {id, turn, flip}` turns a mark's parts' frame for their drawings (quarter turns about z, upside down), kept on the entry, read by `ElementFrames.Of` (the signature reads `ElementFrames.Base`). Prefixes: steel's from the steel members schedule (C, R, GP, P, SR, EB, B, FB, M, BM), PL for plates (`IMarkKinds`), E otherwise. The first numbering keeps the schedule's marks (`SteelMembersSchedule.Marked`, `IMarkKinds.Seeds`), one signature a mark project-wide (a schedule mark that held mirror images keeps it for the first); the schedule shows the register's mark when a row's members all share one, so an old project's schedule reads the same. `{mark}` and `{qty}` are fields of tags, schedules and filters (`TagText`); `drawing.create` numbers its part in its step, the sheet's number is the mark (the user's own number kept), its name "{kind} {mark}", the data table shows the mark and the quantity. Annotate → Number parts runs it. `marks.list` reads the register. Tests: `MarkTests` (6: twenty copies one mark, quantity 20; a mirror image its own; edit one of twenty, edit the only one, a deleted part's mark free then renumbered; 0.5 mm; the hall's schedule marks kept; a drawing's sheet numbered by its mark and a turned frame kept per mark).


**Quantities of references (the user's report "can't get quantities", 14.11.2026, decision 584).** An element imported as a reference (IFC in reference mode or not recognised, Fragments, BIM Open Schema) kept its IFC class, so its class's native quantity producer ran on attributes it does not have: a wall measured 0 m, 0 m², 0 m³, a floor the same, doors and windows were missing from the takeoff and the type was "—", while the file's Qto sets sat unused under IFC properties. `Session.Quantities` now measures every reference in one place before the class's producer (`ReferenceQuantities`, Lapin.Platform): the file's own quantity sets first, typed, in SI by the file's units (the IFC import writes them as `fileQuantities` by the quantity's own unit or the project's unit of its kind, `IfcModel.QuantitiesSi`; Fragments by the scale its storeys are read in; BOS by each parameter's units, an unknown unit not guessed), the base set first; then, for what the file lacks, the stored body's net volume when it is closed and the count, nothing else (`Lapin_BodyQuantities` for a body the file gave none). `ReferenceTypes` gives a reference its type: the native relation, else the file's type record (now named after the type), which the takeoff names, the estimate and the article mapping take as the type and price by its articles; Fragments and BOS give the type's name from their attributes, else ObjectType. The IFC export writes a reference's carried sets only (it wrote native zero sets before), the Fragments export writes a reference's quantity sets as quantities and not again as property sets. Native elements are unchanged. A project imported earlier has no `fileQuantities` and measures by its body until imported again (no upgrade: the file's units are not in the project). Tests: `ReferenceQuantityTests` (IFC round trip, a millimetre file with its own units, a body without quantities, the estimate on a reference's type, Fragments and BOS round trips); guide: Schedules (en, ro).

**Field schedules and field aliases, the engine (14.11.2026, decision 585; the user's request: "users configure their own takeoffs and other schedules from the models' parameters, IFC, Fragments and BOS imports included, and map their office's own property names once").** A new schedule kind, `fields` (`FieldSchedule`, Lapin.Architecture/Schedules), lists the elements a filter takes — the dimensions a work task's rule has (classes, types, storeys, materials, groups, phases, each by id or name) plus conditions on any field (`=`, `≠`, `contains`, `starts with`, `<`, `≤`, `>`, `≥`, `exists`, `missing`; numbers as numbers, texts case aside) (`ElementFilter`, Lapin.Platform/FieldValues.cs) —, one row each or grouped by any fields, with columns of any field or formula added up as asked (sum, avg, min, max, count, countDistinct, first, list), a unit and decimals, a count column and a totals line; its options are the schedule's `fields`. Every value is read through the one field dictionary (`ElementFields`: as a formula reads it when a number, in SI, else as a tag writes it), so the property sets a file gave a reference (`Pset_Firma.CodLucrare`), its quantity sets (`Qto_WallBaseQuantities.Length`), parameters, attributes, scopes and formulas are columns alike; formulas now read imported properties too, and quantities by their set's name. Aliases (`FieldAliases`, on the IfcProject as `fieldAliases`): a name and the fields it stands for in order, the first the element has; tags, live text, formulas, schedules and filters see them, and one named like a standard quantity fills it in for a reference whose file lacks it (`ReferenceQuantities.Standard`), so the takeoff and the estimate measure it. The takeoff's `groupBy` takes any field besides its four dimensions. Queries for the interface: `fields.model` (the fields across the model or a filter's elements, by source, with kind, examples and counts; stored fields counted on every element, the rest on a sample), `fields.values` (one field's distinct values), `fields.read`; commands `fields.setAlias`, `removeAlias`, `setAliases`, `importAliases` and the query `fields.exportAliases` (`.lapinaliases`); `schedule.exportTemplate` and `architecture.importSchedule` (`.lapinschedule`, the filter's ids written as names); both kinds of file go in the firm's folders. Nothing changes for the existing schedules or an older project. The API is in docs/plan-user-extensibility.md §11; the interface is the next step. Tests: `FieldScheduleTests`.

**Gantt charts by the office's own property names, the engine (14.11.2026, decision 586; the user's request: "configuring the Gantt charts should let users configure the parameters when they have other internal standards of property naming", and the answer to 585: planning rules read fields and aliases).** A task's rule (`TaskRule`) takes `conditions` on any field, the field schedules' shape and evaluator (`FieldCondition.ReadAll` / `CheckAll`, now shared by `ElementFilter` and the rule), read through `ElementFields` once per element and field (`TaskElements.Field`), so a user's parameter (`Firma.Sector`), a reference's property set from its file (`Pset_Firma.Etapa`), a quantity, a formula or an alias picks a task's elements, worked out on every ask; the rule travels whole through IFC (`Lapin_Task.Rule`), MS Project XML and the XLSX Rule column. The generator's steps take `conditions`, `splitBy` (a task per value of up to four fields under a summary, named "Masonry · Ground floor · Sector A", ids stable from step, storey and values, the sequence linking the storey's summary), `durationField` (each element's days, summed) and `quantityField` (the quantity under the step's norm in place of the built-in measure); a task takes `durationField`, `quantityField`, `norm` and `crew` on `planning.updateTask`, and `planning.retimeFromFields` works them out again (one undo step). The Gantt's display by fields is kept on the work schedule (`gantt`: `colourBy`, `colours`, `groupBy`, `columns`; `TaskFields`, the task's own `task.*` fields too): `planning.schedule` gives each task its values and colour and the legend, `planning.taskFields` any fields' values for the window's pickers, and the Gantt on a sheet draws the bars in the values' colours (critical ones outlined red), the fields' columns and the legend. The draft model of the generator's preview reads fields through a `ViewQuery` over the session's geometry and quantities. View filters were left as they are: their `param` rule already reads aliases and a file's property sets through `TagText.Value`. Nothing changes without the new keys. The API is in docs/plan-user-extensibility.md §12; the interface is the next step. Tests: `PlanningFieldTests`.

**A solid hatch drawn in its fill colour (forum topic 88, 14.11.2026, decision 587).** A layer whose material was hatched SOLID with a fill colour (insulation in solid yellow) drew in black: the cut's fill went down in the colour, and the solid pattern was then laid over it in the hatch's ink (`Pens.Of`: the category's line colour, else black), hiding it; a line pattern over the same fill showed the colour between its lines, which is why only the solid looked wrong. A solid pattern is now the band's fill: `MaterialHatch` carries the band's `Fill` (its material's, from `MaterialDrawing.Apply`), and `Pens.Of` draws a solid in the category's fill colour, else the material's, else in ink as before, so plans, sections, sheets, PDF and DXF (one path, `Views.Render`) show it; an elevation's surface hatch (`SurfaceHatches`) the same, an element's override colour (a phase's) over it; and the 3D's caps drawn as a sheet (`Session.CapHatch`) fill a solid in the material's fill, else the dark grey as before. Walls and floors go through the same code. A solid without a fill and every other pattern draw as before; the material swatch already showed a solid in its fill colour. The user guide's Materials page says so in both languages. Tests: `SolidHatchColourTests`.

**Corners on outlines, preselection and colour swatches, stage 1 (14.11.2026, decision 588; forum topic 97, "editing a slab's contour, arcs and fillets, preselection highlight, saved colour palettes").** (1) Corner editing was already there (Ctrl/⌘-click on an edge's middle grip adds a vertex, on a corner removes it) but nobody found it. The outline grips now say so in their tooltips, and a right-click on a selected floor, opening, roof, room or balcony offers **Add corner here** (near an edge: the vertex goes on the edge at the point clicked, `addCornerAt` on the edge grips) and **Remove corner** (on a corner; refused under three), each the element's own update command, so one undo step (`GripLayer.cornerItems`, `gripKit.outlineGrips`). (2) Preselection: plans and sections already lit the model element under the cursor; they now light the marks too in plans (drafted shapes, dimensions, tags, symbols, insets), 3D lights the element under the cursor (it only lit a tag's target before), and sheets keep the viewport's dashed hover. File → Preferences → Display has **Preselection highlight** (on by default) and the **Preselection colour** and **Selection colour** (`app/highlight.ts`, `lapin.highlight`; unset means the theme's accent, today's look; the 2D views take them as CSS variables `--hl-pre`, `--hl-select`, 3D through `selectionColour()`/`preselectionColour()`, instanced elements included). (3) One colour field, `shell/ColourField.tsx`, replaces the 38 plain colour inputs: the system's input as before (a value with an opacity keeps it) and a button with the swatches: recent colours (automatic, per user), the **project palette** (the project's `colourPalette`, engine command `project.setColourPalette`, one undo step, travels in the .lapin) and the user's own palettes (`app/colours.ts`, `lapin.colours`: create, rename, move, delete, add and remove colours, reorder them, and "add the colours of the materials in use"), edited in the popover or in Preferences → Colours. Left: palettes are not in the `.lapinstandards` file (a team shares colours through the project palette); arcs and fillets on contours are later stages.

**Curved edges, fillets and chamfers on outlines, forum topic 97 stage 2 (decision 589).** A floor's, a floor opening's and a ceiling's outline edges may be arcs: beside `contour` an optional `arcs` holds each edge's rise off its chord (left of its way positive, the walls' `arc` convention, `PathCurve`); straight outlines keep no `arcs`, so older projects read unchanged. `ArcOutline` (Lapin.Platform) lays the arcs in chords by the one rule for the body, plans and sections, and measures area and perimeter exactly; `Slabs.Corners` is the stored corners (grips, edge indices, extents), `Slabs.Contour` the built outline, `Slabs.Built` maps a curved edge's layer extents onto its chords. New commands `architecture.setEdgeArc`, `roundCorner`, `chamferCorner` (one undo step each); the outline commands take `arcs`; `addSlab {x, y}` makes a floor inside the walls round the point. IFC writes the true outline as a FootPrint IfcIndexedPolyCurve with IfcArcIndex segments beside the B-rep body; `arcs` rides in Lapin_Intent and comes back on import. The UI: an arc grip at a curved edge's middle, right-click Make arc / Make straight / Fillet corner… / Chamfer corner…, and the Floor, Floor opening and Ceiling tools' bar (Polygon with tangent arcs on Space, Rectangle, Circle, Inside walls). Roofs keep straight edges; drawn rooms, balconies and site platforms have their own outline code and no arcs yet. Tests: `CurvedOutlineTests`, `contour.test.ts`, `gripKit.test.ts`, `outlineShape.test.ts`; guide scene `floors-curved`.

**Edit sketch on outlines, forum topic 97 stage 3 (decision 590).** A floor's, a floor opening's or a ceiling's outline opens as a sketch (right-click → Edit sketch, or Studio → Edit sketch): `studio.editOutline` (`OutlineSketches`, Lapin.Studio) makes a plan view of the storey of its own (`outlineSketchOf`, the element hidden there) and draws the outline as lines and arcs held by coincident constraints, or the sketch kept on the element (`outlineSketch`: shapes, constraints, dimensions, and the outline they made, so a grip edit since is noticed and the sketch starts again from the outline). It is edited with the Draft tab and Studio's sketch constraints; `studio.finishOutline` chains the shapes into one closed outline and writes it through the element's own update command in one undo step (id and GlobalId kept), keeping the sketch on the element as design intent (Lapin_Intent); `studio.cancelOutline` drops it. UI: `modules/studio/outlineSketch.tsx` (the bar with Finish sketch and Cancel), `editSketchItems` in the selection menu. What is left (a drafting fillet in a constrained sketch dropping the corner's coincident, 3D editing, parameters, rooms/balconies/platforms) is in `docs/plan-outline-sketch.md`. Tests: `OutlineSketchTests`; guide scene `floors-sketch`.

**Delete on a sheet, symbols on a sheet, Tab through overlapping elements (forum topics 91, 105 and 106, decision 591).** Delete with views active on a sheet takes those views off it in one undo step (Ctrl/Cmd-click adds views) and never the sheet; a sheet is deleted from the project browser. The Place again at another scale prompt has an English source in every language. Symbols placed on a sheet are picked (hover, click, properties), moved by dragging (`drafting.setSymbol`, one undo step) and deleted with Delete, their boxes taken from `symbols.preview`. Tab in a plan, section or elevation with several elements under the pointer lights the next one (Shift+Tab the one before), the status line says "2 of 3: Slab …", and a click takes it; otherwise Tab keeps its meanings. Not done: the 3D view.

**A dog-leg stair as an architect drew it, and its railings one by one, forum topics 104 and 102 (decisions 592 and 593).** Enrique's sketch of a concrete dog-leg asked for the handrail at the railing's height h over the nosings, parallel to the pitch, starting half a tread before the first nosing (dropping there to its post) and ending half a tread past the last one level at h, turning at the landing at h with no gap; and a continuous slab, each flight's soffit running into the landing's. Before, the handrail stopped at the end nosings, climbed gently across the landing (the next flight's rise spread over the whole landing), its bars met at the joints with gaps, and each flight's waist was cut square at the landing's edge, its soffit a notch under the landing's. Now `StairBuilder.RailingPaths` lays the handrail out as asked (`LandingTurn`: level round the outside or across the well, up h/2 half a tread before the next flight), `Railings.Bars` laps each bar over a joint and draws upright steps, and `FlightProfile` carries a flight's soffit on under a landing to the landing's soffit (or down under its first steps to it, leaving one), with no overlapping volume; a type may stand the first flight on a footing of its own (`FootingDepth`, `FootingWidth`, an IfcFooting). Each railing of a stair stays a part of it (an IfcRailing in the IfcStair, BALUSTRADE or HANDRAIL, with `Lapin_StairRailing`) and is selected on its own as `stairId#railing:left|right`, as a grid's axis is: a click in a plan (each railing a mark of its own) or in 3D (the modules' new `subSelectionPick`) selects it, its panel (`StairRailingPanel`, the modules' `subSelectionPanels`) sets its kind (a balustrade or a handrail on the wall, on brackets), height, design and handrail (`architecture.updateStairRailing`; blank, the type's), and Delete takes that side off (`StairDeletion`). Projects saved before open unchanged (no `railings`: the type's, both sides as balustrades). Tests: `StairDogLegTests`, `StairTests` updated for the end posts, `stairRailing.test.ts`; guide: `docs/user/stairs.md` (EN, RO), scene `stair-dogleg-railings`.
**Detail callouts (forum topic 107, decision 594).** View → Detail callout (CO) in a plan, a section or an elevation draws a rectangle round an area (the bar: Circle, Polygon); `view.callout` makes a `LapinCallout` on the view and a detail view of the same kind in one undo step: the parent's settings copied (`calloutOf` names it), cropped to the boundary's box (a crop region, its frame hidden on paper), at 1:20 or the project's next standard scale under the parent's, at LOD 400, named Detail N and listed under Details in the project browser; it is no level's floor plan and draws no section or elevation marker of its own. The parent draws the boundary, a leader and a head with the detail's number and sheet from `SymbolSlots.PlacedOn` ("3 / A-501", "–" before it is placed), the calloutHead slot's symbol when one is chosen (`Callouts.Marks`, in `Views.Render`, so sheets, PDF and DXF print it; Visibility / Graphics' Callouts category hides it). Its grips (`view.updateCallout`) reshape it and crop the detail again; a follow-up (`ICommandFollowUp`) gives the detail the parent's cut, range or line when they change, and moves a rectangle callout when the detail's own crop changes; a reference callout (`reference`) points at a view that is there and makes none. Double-click or right-click → Open view opens the detail; deleting a callout asks whether its detail view goes too, deleting a view takes its callouts. An elevation called out is given a fixed plane first, so the detail stays where it was drawn. Tests: `CalloutTests`, `callouts.test.ts`; guide: views (Detail callouts), sheets; scene `views-callout`.

**Fillet and chamfer on walls and railings, real arcs in railings (a user's request, decisions 592 and 593).** The command line's FILLET and CHAMFER (and Architecture → Fillet / Chamfer, in the Joins group) take two walls as well as two drafted lines: `architecture.filletWalls` / `chamferWalls` (`WallFillets`, Lapin.Architecture) trim or carry on both walls to the tangent points and add between them a curved wall tangent to both (an in-line join at each end) or a straight one (mitred corners), in one undo step; radius 0 is a sharp corner. The part kept is the one picked; the radius is the axis's, or the inside or outside face's (I/O at the prompt). The piece is the first wall again (type, height, base, top constraint and roof attachment, its usage on the same side, the type's sweeps, bottom and top layer extents); the two walls keep their GlobalIds and their openings their place in plan, one falling into the part cut away refusing it; walls that differ are refused unless the first one's is taken (the command line asks Yes/No). Curved walls and walls with a top profile are refused. A wall's end grip joined to another wall offers Fillet corner… and Chamfer corner… on its right-click. Railings keep an optional `arcs` beside `points` (each segment's rise, left positive), built by the one rule for curves: the handrail, rails and glass in chords, the posts and balusters spaced along each whole segment (`Railings.Solids` takes the segment of each chord), the length exact, the plan a band round the arc; `setRailingArc`, `roundRailingCorner`, `chamferRailingCorner`, and `filletRailings` / `chamferRailings` (two segments of one railing, or two railings made one round the arc). The railing's grips have an arc grip and Make arc / Make straight / Fillet corner / Chamfer corner. Draft → Convert → Railing keeps an arc and makes one railing of lines, arcs and polylines meeting end to end (`addRailing` with `join`). IFC writes a railing's true curve as its Axis (IfcIndexedPolyCurve with IfcArcIndex). Stair and balcony railings are unchanged. Tests: `WallFilletTests`, `RailingArcTests`, `session.test.ts` (FILLET on walls and railings), `Railing.test.ts`; guide scene `walls-fillet`.

**Array on drafting, forum topic 90 (decision 597).** Modify → Array did nothing on a drafted rectangle: the tool's `arrayable()` kept only elements on a storey and groups. The engine's `element.array` already carried drafted shapes, texts and symbols as `element.transform` does (`AnnotationPlacement`, the view's own points), so the copies of a drafted shape are a `LapinArray` like a wall's, edited in the same Array section (count, step, grid, centre, path; one undo step each), and a path can be a drafted line of the view. The UI now decides what it takes where (`arrayTargets` in `modules/core/arrayGrid.ts`): in a plan, elements and drafting together (they share the model's x and y); in a section, an elevation or a drafting view only the drafting (elements are arrayed in a plan or in 3D, as their turns would tip them over there); in 3D only the elements; dimensions and tags never (they measure or name their elements); what is left out is said. Array is on the Draft tab's Modify panel and the command line (`ARRAY`). Tests: `DraftArrayTests` (a drafted rectangle in a row: count, positions, array.update, undo; grid, polar text, along a drafted line edited; in a section's own coordinates; a wall and a rectangle together), `arrayGrid.test.ts`; guide scene `array-draft`.

**Dimensions follow stairs and what a plan does not cut, forum topic 102 (decision 598).** A dimension's point held only on what the plan cuts (`Dimensions.Associate` read `ViewDrawing.Cuts`), so a dimension to a stair's corner (a stair is drawn by its marks), a beam seen overhead, a floor's edge, furniture, a railing or a Studio solid below the cut was a free point and stayed put. The drawing now carries the outlines a module draws as marks (`ViewDrawing.Outlines`, from the plan items of kind `outline`: a stair's whole outline, never drawn from there), and a point that is on no cut edge (nor a grid axis) holds on an element's line or outline, as an "edge" end, kept in the element's frame (`Dimensions.Frame`): its axis, its placement and rotation, now read in degrees as every element keeps it (it was read in radians, so a turned column lost its dimensions), its outline's first side (a floor, a ceiling) or its path's first piece (a railing). Ends kept the old way carry no `frame` and are brought into today's frame where they are as a project opens (`DimensionFrameUpgrade`). Element.transform did not move a railing at all (its 2D points were not carried): `Railings` now has its own placement. Before: walls, openings, grid axes and the cut of a column followed a move; a column's turn lost its point; stairs, beams overhead, floors, furniture, railings and Studio solids seen below did not follow. Now all of them follow a move, a turn and an edit (a stair made wider moves the point on its side). `drafting.dimension` also returns its points as drawn and what each holds on. Tests: `DimensionFollowTests`.

**The free edition audit, stage 1: its bugs fixed (15.11.2026, decision 599; `docs/audit-2026-11-15.md`).** Seven batches, one per area, each with its tests. *Saving*: a project saves with its versions and issued files after its file is renamed, moved or deleted while open, and recovers from the journal then too (a private copy of the open file, `PackageBases`); versions or other entries that could not be read are carried over byte for byte, or the save over the file is refused, never silently dropped; damaged version parts and issued files are checked by hash and left out with a warning; a save is flushed to disk before it replaces the project; nothing is written to the file during a script's open step; a failing journal no longer loses an edit's diff; a version compared with the project shows no change that comes only from an upgrade; an IFC export never overwrites a file the project links or was imported from; the host answers every call without dropping the window. *Numbers*: `Json.Number` reads NaN, Infinity and too-large numbers as none and 62 range checks are written so NaN fails them; a rebar zone's step is 10 mm at least, marks, arrays and distributions are capped, a contour interval is 5 cm at least and contour levels stop where the interval no longer moves the height, a planting follows its class's density and a zone grows at most 5 000 plants checked on a grid, configurator previews refuse huge samples;
